#131. Robot vessels take on the dangerous work of mine hunting

This South Korean vessel is among the ships damaged in the Strait of Hormuz

Traversing the Strait of Hormuz remains one of the most dangerous trips a vessel can take.

Despite assistance from the US Navy, ships are still being attacked.

"To transit the Strait of Hormuz you need agreement between the captain and the crew to take the risk. You need war risk insurance. And the captain has to make the final decision," says Steve Cameron, a former merchant navy officer and maritime consultant.

Missiles and drones are a threat from the air, but under the surface mines may also be lurking.

"If you're a captain, you'd like to see evidence mines aren't present," Cameron adds.

Modern mines rest on the seabed and detonate when they detect an acoustic or magnetic signal, triggering a pressure wave that will break the keel of a passing vessel.

Others can rise through shallow waters or float, or be powered towards a target.

The technology itself is not difficult or costly, but the mere threat can stop a tanker fleet from sailing.

Ian McFarlane of French defence group Thales is a naval architect who knows mine warfare well. "Mines are a very cheap way of waging a naval war, as the Royal Navy is acutely aware," he says.

Finding sea mines has traditionally been the job of specialist mine-hunting vessels, with crews of around 80 sailors.

To keep humans out of harm's way, an increasing amount of the work is done by systems that are autonomous and monitored from the shore.

They might only need 20 personnel, working in shifts on land. With the recruitment and retention challenges facing Nato navies these highly-portable robot systems are very attractive.

Such tech has been under development for 20 years.

During the 2003 Iraq war the US Navy poked around the seabed looking for mines with a torpedo-shaped robot called the Remus 100.

Pinging around with sonar these little machines operated down to around 100ft depth and spared divers a difficult and dangerous task.

Iran's military published this picture of a US submersible captured in the Gulf

The latest conflict in the Gulf is testing much more capable systems.

The US military is sparing with details about their mine-hunting craft, but we have seen glimpses of them.

Iranian forces hauled an uncrewed US autonomous sub, external out of the waters of the Strait of Hormuz in early September.

The Pentagon claimed this robot, an Extra Large Uncrewed Underwater Vehicle (XLUUV) from US autonomous defence tech venture Anduril, was a malfunctioning older device with no classified equipment on-board.

Whatever its mission, no sailors were imperilled. That, says William Lupton, a naval analyst with Shephard Group, "was a drop in the ocean compared to having prisoners taken", and part of the point of uncrewed vessels.

What if an uncrewed vessel is captured? Nato navies are reluctant to go into details, but computer drives can be wiped or destroyed remotely by various means. This may have taken place inside the humming circuit boards of the XLUUV seized by Iran.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle is packed with sensors

Such autonomous tech is being embraced by forces around the world.

Cooperation between Thales, the Royal Navy, and France's Marine Nationale has produced a robot motor launch.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle (USV) is packed with sensors programmed to detect mines via sonar that was developed for the oil and gas industries.

It tows a 5m long rectangular box, similar to a large torpedo, that takes a hi-resolution look at the seabed and creates a 3D picture refined by AI. The latest mines are designed to blend into their surroundings and the software spots anything that looks like a mine.

Autonomous systems are cheaper than conventional minehunters, plus they can be deployed, along with containerised control centres, by air.

Steve Prest, associate fellow at the RUSI defence think tank, introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy and was its first fleet robotics officer, a title he acquired in 2014.

"There is a bunch of ways to look for a mine using robot boats or robot subs, and while you can't prove the negative, that no mines are there, you can reduce the risk."

He says US robot minehunters can either blow up a mine or just mark the spot as somewhere to avoid.

"We'll see more use of autonomy but also the missions and capability will expand. And AI will be analysing data from those vessels on the spot as opposed to data being analysed when a drone returns."

The Royal Navy recently announced its concept of a hybrid navy - mixing drones with conventional warships. But Prest thinks for this vision to succeed the fleet must operate as one distributed force, sharing data in what he terms a cognitive architecture.

The robot only makes a real difference as part of this scheme and it's a mistake to focus on individual uncrewed assets. "Uncrewed or autonomous vessels are not the operating system. They are applications running on it."

Steve Prest introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy

While robots are on the rise, people are what really matter in the Strait.

Neil Roberts speaks for insurers at Lloyd's of London on marine matters and says the psychological impact of the mine threat is inescapable, as is the uncertainty around just how many mines, if any, are out there.

"The US says it has cleared the Omani route and believes the mine threat is being managed, with shipments running at about 75% of pre-conflict levels."

Fear of the unknown is the most potent aspect of the sea mine threat and robots are now the weapon of choice to confront that fear.

#130. Skydance outlines its $6 billion in planned cost savings. Here's the breakdown

Skydance is pulling back the curtain on how it plans to achieve at least $6 billion in cost savings in the next three years.

"By combining two global streaming services, two film and TV studio groups, two television portfolios, and overlapping corporate functions, we can reduce duplicative spend across an expense base of more than $50 billion," the company wrote in a letter to shareholders Monday as part of Paramount Skydance's third-quarter earnings release.

Skydance, the company formed by the combination of Paramount Skydance and Warner Bros. Discovery, said it projects at least $2.2 billion in cost reductions from eliminating "redundant back-office and corporate functions," including eliminating management layers and ramping the use of artificial intelligence. Those savings also account for "rationalizing" its office footprint.

Skydance expects at least $1 billion in savings from streamlining marketing and promotional spending. And around $1.4 billion in savings will come from unifying its streaming businesses and broadcast architecture and integrating back-end technology.

A final $1.4 billion in savings will be tied to "optimizing third-party spend," eliminating overlapping expenses, and consolidating purchasing decisions to one centralized group, Skydance said.

"We are confident in achieving these savings, drawing on our playbook from Paramount," the company wrote. "That program is on track to achieve over $2.7 billion in run-rate efficiencies by the end of 2026."

Some of that cost-cutting has already begun.

Last month, Skydance began offering voluntary buyouts to employees ahead of official layoffs. At the same time, the company adopted a five-day-per-week in-office policy. Additionally, last week saw the departure of eight executives — four from Warner Bros. and four from Paramount.

The company has previously said it plans to eventually merge the Paramount+ and HBO Max streaming services into a single platform.

On Monday, Skydance said it would take the first step toward that goal early next year, offering a bundle of the streaming services "at an even greater value."

Co-CEO Ynon Kreiz told investors during the company's earnings call that Skydance is also looking for ways to optimize its content budget. The company spends more than $30 billion annually on shows and films.

"As one company, we can make smarter choices about how we distribute and monetize both new content and library content," Kreiz said.

Skydance noted that it's expecting a $200 million headwind in the fourth quarter from theatrical underperformances at Warner Bros. studio related to already launched titles and upcoming releases.

The company will also utilize AI to speed up production and decrease costs to make films, co-CEO and chairman David Ellison said on the call.

"We think these tools will help unlock more original storytelling by being able to make the budget more digestible to be able to take that amount of risk," Ellison said.

The updates came alongside third-quarter results for the legacy Paramount Skydance company that included revenue of $7.03 billion, which was roughly in line with StreetAccount estimates of $7.04 billion, and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $1 billion, topping StreetAccount estimates of $913.5 million.

However, adjusted earnings per share of 45 cents were not immediately comparable to StreetAccount EPS estimates.

The results don't include the impact of the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, which closed after the end of the third quarter.

Skydance detailed business units of the combined entity, including studios, direct-to-consumer and TV, and said it will provide full-year guidance during its fourth-quarter earnings report early next year.

"Going forward, we will provide guidance on an annual basis, as we prioritize long-term value creation, which we believe will be more consistent with our strategic approach," the company told shareholders. "We will continue to provide quarterly updates, including certain forward-looking commentary where applicable, which will give investors visibility into our near-term progress of the business."

#128. Nissan cuts sales forecast again on China woes as CEO outlines plan to fight back

Nissan Motor plans to expand exports of vehicles developed in China to help compete against Chinese rivals globally, CEO Ivan Espinosa told CNBC on Wednesday.

The export push comes as intensifying competition in the world's largest auto market forced the company to cut its annual sales forecast for a second time in months.

Espinosa said Nissan would rapidly increase its exports of China-developed vehicles from two models to five, targeting 12 regional market clusters in the "very near term."

The Japanese automaker is responding to intensifying competition in China by developing vehicles specifically for Chinese consumers, using local technology, lower costs and faster development processes, he said.

"We can defend ourselves from these very aggressive players by using exactly the same elements that they have: speed, cost, and good technology," he told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

Nissan faces weakening demand and an accelerating shift toward electric vehicles in China, even as its restructuring efforts show progress in the U.S. and Japan.

On Wednesday, Nissan cut its global sales outlook for the fiscal year ending March 2027 to between 2.97 million and 3 million vehicles, down from 3.15 million previously. It maintained its full-year operating profit and net cash guidance.

In China, "weaker demand, a faster-than-anticipated shift to [new energy vehicles] and intensifying competition are putting greater pressure on sales than initially anticipated," the company said in a statement.

In August, Nissan revised its full-year sales volume outlook from 3.3 million units to 3.15 million units.

In October, shares of peer Volvo Cars crashed after it reported a swing to operating loss, citing tough market conditions in China and the U.S.

CEO Håkan Samuelsson told CNBC at the time that it needed a new strategy in China, with regionally adapted cars.

"It's really a lot about the Chinese market, of course, where the change has come faster than we thought, and really now we can see that selling global cars developed in Europe and China will not work, and then this is affecting all the Western players in China," Samuelsson said.

On Wednesday, Nissan also reported a 96% drop in second-quarter operating profit to 2.1 billion yen ($13 million), significantly below analyst expectations of 54.9 billion yen, according to FactSet.

The drop in profits was due to no longer having the benefit of lower U.S. emissions-related expenses that boosted profits a year ago, the company said.

Net revenue grew 8.2% to 3.1 trillion yen in the quarter, largely in line with consensus.

The quarterly results came after the Tokyo market closed. Shares are down 29% year-to-date.

Nissan's Frankfurt-listed shares dropped 4% in early afternoon trading.

Espinosa, who has led the company since April 2025, also emphasised progress on a turnaround plan to reduce its global manufacturing footprint and cut its workforce by 15%. About 18 months into the plan, the company has achieved 365 billion yen in savings, Espinosa said.

#126. Sales of tinned food and bottled water jump after 'Be Ready' campaign launch

Sales of essential goods such as bottled water and canned food soared in the 10 days following the launch of the government's "Be Ready" emergency preparedness campaign on 22 October, research indicates.

Hand sanitiser sales were up 83%, sales of bottled water rose 67% and tinned and jarred goods climbed 37%, according to data from retail research firm Worldpanel by Numerator.

The government's public awareness campaign encouraged people to stock up in case of disruption to essential services by severe weather or cyberattacks.

However, Worldpanel said that while households were stockpiling essentials, they were not panic buying.

The government campaign encouraged people to keep three days-worth of food and water in their homes.

Wet wipes, a torch and cash were also recommended.

After the campaign launch, the equivalent of an extra 400,000 people per day bought tinned meat, Worldpanel said, although the typical amount remained at 500g, which suggests a gradual stocking-up.

"Shoppers clearly took notice of the 'Be Ready' campaign, but this wasn't panic buying, with less than a third of households buying bottled water in October," said Fraser McKevitt, head of retail and consumer insight at Worldpanel by Numerator.

"Growth came from more people adding the recommended items on the government's checklist to their weekly shop, not from shoppers piling their trolleys high, which is very much in line with what the government wanted households to do," he said.

Sales of tinned and jarred goods rose from Â£58.2m in the 10 days to 2 November 2025 to Â£79.5m over the same period in 2026.

Bottled water sales jumped from Â£13.6m to Â£22.8m compared with a year earlier and hand sanitiser sales climbed from Â£497,000 to Â£908,000.

Further evidence of stockpiling came from food firm Princes on Tuesday, which said demand for tinned tuna, meat and vegetables had risen following the government's campaign.

Princes interim chief executive Giuseppe Mastrolia said the jump in demand "further reinforces the relevance of our core categories in providing affordable, nutritious and convenient food solutions".

The "Be Ready" campaign warns that severe weather and cyber attacks could disrupt essential services, and British households need to make sure they have what they need - for example, bottled water, and long-life food that doesn't need to be kept in the fridge such as dried fruit and nuts.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has said everyone should have "those supplies in the house to get you through those moments".

Disaster recovery expert Lucy Easthope previously told the BBC that the UK was playing "massive catch up" to other European nations in terms of preparedness.

These include France, Germany Denmark, Sweden, and Norway.

A number of experts have been calling for the UK to put more emphasis on food security, with some arguing that the government itself should take responsibility for food stockpiling, rather than individual households.

In October, a group of more than 180 experts signed an open letter, external to Burnham and first secretary of state Louise Haigh calling for local authorities to have a duty to prevent and respond to food supply emergencies.

They pointed out that a government survey found that 29% of people in the UK could not afford to keep a few days worth of food in the house.

Have you been stockpiling? If so, what have you been buying?

#124. General strikes to paralyze Spain as protestors clash with police ahead of election campaign

Spanish workers are expected to turn out in the millions on Wednesday for the country's first general strike in 14 years, as anger over soaring rents and living costs intensifies less than three weeks before a general election. Demonstrators clashed with police at a wholesale market in Madrid in the early hours of the strike, while roadblocks were also reported in the early hours of Wednesday. The 24-hour labor walkout is expected to disrupt public transport and flights, as well as bring some businesses, shops and factories to a standstill.

Housing costs are a particular source of frustration among demonstrators. Rents have almost doubled over the past decade, squeezing household budgets as many workers feel homeownership is beyond their reach. The Bank of Spain estimates that the country faces a shortage of 750,000 homes, potentially rising to 1 million over the next two years. Spain's two largest trade unions, CCOO and UGT, which called the strike, are calling for greater government intervention in the housing market. Housing activists demand an end to tourist rentals and automatic lease renewals, while unions are seeking greater housing intervention and a new minimum-wage agreement.

UGT Secretary-General Pepe Álvarez urged members to join the protests, saying "there is no salary that can cope with the price of housing in this country," in comments translated by CNBC. He added that it was time for the government to listen to citizens' demands.

The strike follows weeks of social unrest. The eviction of an 87-year-old woman from her Madrid home, followed by her death around two weeks later, sparked national outrage. The left-wing government's attempts to pass measures to help tenants were blocked by the opposition.

Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez's coalition responded by calling an election for Nov. 29, hoping to bolster his support and secure another four years in power. But a high-speed train collision that killed 69 people, the second such disaster this year, shifted the debate toward public safety and accusations of government mismanagement.

The government has not officially endorsed the strike, whose slogan is "Salaries. Housing. Prices." But the protests are widely seen as an opportunity for left-wing parties, including Sánchez's Spanish Socialist Workers' Party, to put housing at the center of the election campaign. Political scientist Oriol Bartomeus of the Autonomous University of Barcelona told The Associated Press that a strong, peaceful turnout could help the left return the campaign to a focus on housing. Sánchez took to social media earlier this week to voice support for existing policies including national health insurance and a 40-hour workweek, warning that a right-wing government would introduce austerity measures and cuts to social services.

"Your eight-hour working day. Your pensions. Your public healthcare. None of this fell from the sky. They were collective achievements," he told voters on Monday.

The conservative Popular Party and far-right Vox, which already govern in some parts of Spain, accuse the unions of organizing the protests to keep the current government in power. "The strike is not a general strike, it's an election strike," Miguel Tellado, a spokesperson for the Popular Party, said Tuesday.

Both parties oppose intervention in the housing market.

Opinion polls suggest a shift to the right, with the Popular Party leading and potentially able to form a government with Vox.

Such a coalition would bring the far right into Spain's national government for the first time since Francisco Franco's 36-year dictatorship ended in 1975.

A poll for El País and Cadena SER, published Nov. 9, put the Popular Party at 31.9%, Sánchez's Partido Socialista Obrero Español at 29.9% and Vox at 18%. The survey, conducted Nov. 2-4, projected a combined 190 seats for the Popular Party and Vox, above the 176 needed for a majority in the 350-seat lower house. A strong turnout at Wednesday's protests could prove crucial for the government, whose handling of the migration crisis in Ceuta drew widespread criticism. Sánchez's family has also been plagued by corruption scandals.

The prime minister's personal standing has suffered. Some 58% of respondents had a "bad" or "very bad" opinion of him, compared with 41% who had a good or very good opinion, according to a poll for elDiario.es conducted Nov. 2-5.

Violence during the strike, however, could backfire on the government. Protests in France in October were seen as an opportunity for the country's far-right party to emphasize its ability to restore order and security ahead of the country's 2027 presidential elections.

"On the evening of 11 November, there will almost certainly be two narratives: one of resounding success and one of failure. Neither should be read as an exit poll. It will be more useful to look at other things: which sectors and regions stop work, how many people take to the streets and, above all, whether housing moves to the centre of the campaign," wrote Maite Aurrekoetxea, professor of social and human sciences at the University of Deusto, in comments translated by Google.

#123. Claude chatbot maker set for $1.3tn stock market debut despite 'catastrophic' AI warning

Anthropic, the artificial intelligence (AI) company behind the Claude chatbot, could be valued at around $1.3 trillion (Â£1 trillion) when it lists its shares for sale on New York's Nasdaq stock exchange.

The loss-making company says it will offer its shares for between $70 and $78 each and will raise up to $64bn when it floats this month.

It would value Anthropic at between $1.1tn and $1.3tn which, although large, is below the $2tn some backers of the firm predicted in summer, according to the Financial Times.

The huge valuation comes despite the company warning last month that AI could pose "catastrophic or existential risks to humanity".

It is floating at a volatile time for big tech firms. After last week's US midterm elections, the Nasdaq fell sharply on concerns a Democrat-led Congress could slow development of AI and the buildout of data centres.

There are also wider fears over the billions of dollars being spent on AI and whether it will pay off.

In its most recent results for the six months to the end of June, Anthropic's revenues jumped to $16.2bn from $1.1bn in the same period last year.

However, its pre-tax loss jumped to $80.7bn from $12.9bn.

Fiona Cincotta, a senior market analyst at City Index, said the $64bn Anthropic wants to raise is "fairly bullish" within "the volatility [and] concerns towards AI that we've seen in sort of particularly last week".

She told Reuters: "It's showing that they are still sort of upbeat about the outlook as far as AI is concerned and that obviously that they think that the demand is going to be there."

Anthropic, which will list under the symbol ANT on 19 November, will be the first fully AI-focused company to go public, making it a test case for the future of the technology.

At $1.3tn, Anthropic's possible valuation is below SpaceX, Elon Musk's rocket, satellite and artificial intelligence company, which listed its shares in June and was valued at nearly $1.8tn.

Global stocks plunge as fears over US AI slowdown spread to Asia and Europe

Anthropic's blockbuster share listing - 5 surprising things we now know

Anthropic is just five years old. It was co-founded in 2021 by brother and sister Dario and Daniela Amodei along with five others â all of whom worked at OpenAI, the rival AI giant behind ChatGPT.

It announced its intention to list on the stock market in June and OpenAI followed suit. That listing is now expected to occur early next year.

The listing announcements were followed by a series of incidents involving AI agents, which are bots designed to operate somewhat autonomously, going rogue.

Current and former staff of both companies have warned AI technology was being developed without the ability to protect humanity from possible future harms enacted by AI.

Anthropic has admitted that advanced AI systems "could, if not developed, deployed, and operated with appropriate safeguards, oversight, and security measures, pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity, particularly if such systems were misused, replicated, weaponised, or allowed to operate with a high degree of autonomy".

The company has reiterated promises to develop AI to be beneficial to people.

It has warned investors its future success depends largely on its ability to develop the most advanced AI models, and that "any failure to do so could adversely affect our business".

#120. American soldier arrested on suspicion of spying for Russia

A senior soldier in the U.S. Army has been arrested in the U.K. on suspicion of spying for Russia, London's Metropolitan Police said on Tuesday evening.

The unnamed 41-year-old man was arrested near Gloucester, a city in western England, on Tuesday as part of an investigation by the U.K.'s Counter Terrorism Policing. British police worked with U.S. authorities, including the military and the FBI, in their investigations.

A senior NATO military official confirmed on Tuesday that the American service member was assigned to the military alliance's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps (ARRC).

ARRC, headquartered in the English county of Gloucestershire, is a  3-star NATO headquarters with more than 400 permanent staff from more than 20 nations ready to deploy land forces where they are needed.

Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told reporters on Wednesday that Moscow had "absolutely nothing to do with" the case in the U.K.

The individual at the center of the case, detained in London, is suspected of committing offenses under Sections 1 and 3 of the U.K.'s National Security Act.

Section 1 offenses relate to obtaining or disclosing protected information, while Section 3 involves assisting a foreign intelligence service.

The maximum penalty is life imprisonment for a Section 1 offense and 14 years' imprisonment for a Section 3 offense. A fine may also be imposed for either offense.

Police officers are carrying out searches at two locations in Gloucestershire, the Metropolitan Police added.

The arrest comes as Washington is seeking to broker an end to Russia's war in Ukraine. U.S.-mediated talks between Russian and Ukrainian officials began in Abu Dhabi on Tuesday, the first such meeting since February, while Kyiv and its European allies continue to press for credible security guarantees as part of any settlement.

President Donald Trump, a longstanding critic of NATO and its members' defense budgets, said on Monday that there appeared to be a growing willingness on both sides to end the almost five-year war.

Russia has long threatened retaliatory action against an expansion of NATO's eastern flank and has previously demanded guarantees that Ukraine will never be allowed to join the alliance. Finland and Sweden became NATO's newest members in 2023 and 2024, respectively.

Since Russia and Ukraine have been at war, Russian activity has also been reported deeper into European and NATO territory, with regional authorities investigating whether Moscow could be involved in some of the recent disarray seen across the continent.

FBI Director Kash Patel said in a statement on Tuesday evening that the allegations against a U.S. serviceman were "of the utmost concern to the United States and our allies."

"I want to send a clear message – this FBI has no tolerance for anyone who breaks their oath to the United States and whose actions endanger our national security and that of our partners," he said.

Sean Stinchon, the commanding general of U.S. Army Counterintelligence Command, said in a statement that "the alleged conduct of a senior solider, entrusted with sensitive information, is a sobering reminder that insider threats are real and persistent."

Authorities said Tuesday that the investigation is not related to any other ongoing Counter Terrorism Policing investigations.

On Oct. 27, British prosecutors said they had charged a man with assisting a foreign intelligence service over an incident at a military base used by the U.S. for strikes on Iran. The base in question, RAF Fairford, is in Gloucestershire, around 30 miles from ARRC's headquarters at Imjin Barracks.

#118. Nvidia hires major US law firm to investigate alleged Chinese spy affair

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has become the face of the US AI boom

Nvidia has hired one of the largest law firms in the US to investigate the affair between its own chief executive and an alleged Chinese spy.

Jensen Huang, who co-founded the tech company, has admitted to having a romantic relationship outside of his marriage. The woman, Alice Huang, is alleged to be a spy for Beijing.

Nvidia said on Tuesday that it had hired New York law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz, and that its attorney Joon Kim would lead "a thorough and independent investigation". The FBI is also conducting its own probe.

The company's share price has fallen almost to a yearly low, with $500bn (Â£375bn) wiped of its stock market value since Friday.

Nvidia is widely considered one of the most important companies behind the artificial intelligence (AI) boom, given it is the sole manufacturer of a computer chip used in AI tools.

It is the world's second most valuable company after Apple and its chips have become a matter of national security for US officials who are keen to win the AI race.

Alice Huang, who is also known as Huang Le, and is not related to Jensen Huang, was an executive at Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has been a customer of Nvidia.

Megaspeed had been suspected of helping divert Nvidia chips to China, a concern that Nvidia has said was investigated and debunked.

Nvidia has also maintained that the US government did not inform it that Megaspeed posed an intelligence concern.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

According to the New York Times, Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington earlier this year warning that Jensen Huang was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

Beijing has rejected claims that the Nvidia chief has been targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "intended to smear and malign China".

US President Donald Trump said on Monday that he had spoken to Jensen Huang, who had said he had "no idea she was a spy." Trump said he believed him.

However, the relationship has drawn more political attention. Congressional leaders of the House Committee on Foreign Affairs have asked Huang to appear next week for a closed door briefing.

The Nvidia boss has yet to say whether he will attend, a person familiar with the committee's demand told the BBC. The committee has the authority to subpoena Huang, effectively forcing him to appear before it, if deemed necessary.

When announcing on Sunday that it would investigate the affair, Nvidia's board said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

It added that a committee of independent directors would oversee the probe.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Wachtell is one of the largest law firms in the US and has several notable clients in the tech industry. Among them are OpenAI and Salesforce.

Joon Kim is a former US Attorney for New York's Southern District and spent several years working as a government attorney focusing on fraud and other criminal acts.

The Nvidia investigation will likely be treated as urgent, according to sources familiar with the matter, as Jensen Huang's public image is tightly intertwined with the company.

He has become a key public figure in the development on AI, arguing the technology is a "new industrial revolution" and is on par with the advent of electricity.

It is unclear, however, if Wachtell will be able to interview Alice Huang.

A spokesperson for Nvidia declined to comment on Tuesday when asked if Jensen Huang had retained any of his own legal representation amid the company investigation and the separate one launched by the FBI.

#117. How GM is trying to talk used car shoppers into buying new vehicles

Affordability challenges are pushing some buyers out of the new vehicle market. Many are choosing used cars instead.

In turn, automakers are looking for ways to compete with many of the online retailers grabbing market share in the used car market and trying to bring some of those customers back into a new vehicle.

New vehicle sales tend to be more profitable for automakers, but reselling trade-ins as used vehicles is highly profitable for dealers. Used car prices are relatively strong, but newer online retailers such as Carvana and CarMax have made the market more competitive for dealers and other incumbent used car sellers.

"The internet and technology and apps on our phone have changed every aspect of retail, and probably one of the slowest areas of retail to have adopted to online and digital shopping has been the auto industry," said Ivan Feinseth, partner, chief investment officer and director of research at Tigress Financial Partners.

General Motors is a key example of automakers' attempts to address this shifting market. The automaker has revamped its certified used vehicle program, which now operates under the CarBravo name. It's essentially an online portal through which Chevrolet, Buick and GMC dealers can list their certified used vehicles and make them available to customers all over the U.S.

About 1,615 of GM's 3,700 dealers are enrolled in the program, according to the company. The only GM brand not involved is Cadillac, which maintains its own program.

"Certified" used or "preowned" vehicles are used cars that have undergone a thorough inspection and reconditioning. They typically come with guarantees and warranties from the manufacturer.

Many automakers have certified used programs for their own cars. But GM, as part of its revamp, is using CarBravo to certify vehicles made by other automakers. A lot of those cars arrive at dealerships as trade-ins, and often dealers will sell them to wholesale auctions, said John Fitzpatrick, senior manager of the CarBravo program.

"Wholesaling doesn't bring nearly the revenue that selling it to a new customer does," he said.

GM has said it is planning to net about 250,000 sales in its CarBravo program in 2026. Fitzpatrick said he doesn't expect that even half of those will end up being new car sales.

But through CarBravo, the customer gets access to GM's dealership network for service, something Fitzpatrick says is a key differentiator between its program and growing online sellers. CarBravo vehicles come with various warranties, depending on the age and mileage on the car.

GM said the program is driving more traffic into local dealerships, where purchases are typically finalized. That creates an opportunity to convert a used car shopper into a new car buyer. So far, the automaker said about 38% of the shoppers initially looking for used cars end up buying new.

A customer who wants to buy a full-size truck can look on the website and compare used Chevrolet Silverados alongside used Ford F-150s and Ram 1500s, for example. The buyer might settle on a used truck from any of those brands, but a conversation with a dealer can push them in a different direction.

"The customer picks up the phone, but in the back of their mind, they maybe always have been a traditional, more new car customer," Fitzpatrick said.

"You've shown the customer, you're going to pay $500 [per month] for this used F-150, but you may pay $510 or $515 for this new Chevy," he said. "And now all of a sudden the customer says, yeah, maybe I can do that because I'd rather have that new vehicle than the used truck."

While the average transaction price of a new vehicle is about $50,000, the average 3-year-old used car costs about $30,000 as of September, according to Cox Automotive.

However, industry analysts say customers base their purchasing decisions on the cost of the monthly payment more than on the sticker price of the car.

New cars often come with longer-term loan options than used ones, Feinseth said, which can result in lower monthly payments.

"On a 1- or 2-year-old car, you may be only stuck with a 3- or 4-year loan," he said. "On a new car, you can get a 5-year, 6-year loan."

The share of new car loans lasting 84 months hit a new record in the third quarter, at more than 1 in 4 loans, according to Edmunds.

Meanwhile, used car prices in some affordable segments have risen, according to Cox Automotive, and about three quarters of consumers express some interest in certified cars.

But the key is building the relationship with the customer, he said. About 50% of the buyers GM has gained through the program appear to be new to the brand, meaning there is no record of them in any of the automaker's customer databases. About 70% had never visited the dealership they ended up purchasing from.

"The goal is really to get that customer engaged with the store, give them the option of building a relationship with the dealer," he said. "The dealer can really help the customer figure out what works for their budget."

It's a boon for dealers too: Customers buying warranty-backed used cars often return to the dealership for service visits — which often account for about half of a dealership's profits. GM can also sell subscriptions to connected vehicle services, such as its OnStar, which provides safety, security and navigation services.

"That is a big part of General Motors' business going forward, is to sell the software and subscription services like OnStar and eventually to the cars that are enabled with Super Cruise," GM's advanced driver-assistance system, Feinseth said.

GM isn't the only automaker trying to use its certified preowned vehicle program to lure buyers.

Detroit rival Ford Motor also certifies vehicles made by other manufacturers through its Ford Blue Advantage program. It is on track to do 450,000 total sales in 2026, more than GM's 250,000 target. It didn't specify how many of those buyers end up purchasing new vehicles.

Toyota told CNBC in an email that about 40% of its used car shoppers end up buying new instead.

Honda didn't give specifics about its program. But Lance Woelfer, American Honda's vice president of automobile sales, said conversions happen "probably every day at dealerships throughout the country."

"But it also is more about that first interaction with the consumer," Woelfer said. "And there are people in any market that prefer to buy a used vehicle, but appreciate the confidence that a [certified preowned designation] can give them."

Woelfer said those customers come back to get their vehicles serviced at a rate about 18% higher than someone who buys a non-certified used car. They also return to purchase a new car at a rate about 7% to 8% higher in the future, he said.

"It's about that first relationship that allows us to then have that customer loyalty for life," he added. "And that's where our focus is. We've been doing it a long time, and we've seen a lot of benefits from the certified business."

#114. Bessent reportedly moved to military base over Iranian threats on his life

U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was reportedly moved from his Washington, D.C., home to a military base due to Iranian threats on his life.

The New York Times, citing officials familiar with the matter, said that U.S. officials had concluded Iranian plotting against current and former American officials had reached a more advanced stage.

Separately, Reuters reported that the severity of the threats against Bessent was not immediately clear.

Bessent has led Operation Economic Outcast, a Treasury campaign launched in August to isolate Iran economically through sanctions and other financial measures, in addition to the U.S. military actions.

The Times reported that Bessent's role in that campaign has made him one of the Trump administration officials Iran most wanted to target.

CNBC has contacted the White House and the U.S. Department of the Treasury for comment.

The move comes as the Department of Justice said late Tuesday that three Iranian nationals had been indicted on terrorism-related charges.

The department said the three were allegedly involved in nearly 20 attacks and attempted attacks across Europe and the United States.

The alleged plots included a September 2026 attack targeting RAF Fairford in the U.K., where U.S. military personnel were stationed.

"Anyone who plans a terrorist plot on a military base housing American service members will be prosecuted by the United States government," Attorney General Todd Blanche said.

Earlier in October, British Prime Minister Andy Burnham said Iran was involved in the incident at RAF Fairford that led to the arrest of five men on suspicion of explosives and terrorism offenses.

Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi then rejected the allegation, writing on X that Burnham was "barking up the wrong tree."

RAF Fairford, in Gloucestershire, is a key U.S. Air Force operating location in Europe and has supported deployments of American strategic bombers and other aircraft.

Separately, U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio reportedly said military action against Iran may not be necessary if economic sanctions and Washington's naval blockade are working.

On Monday, President Donald Trump said on Truth Social that "Qatar, Saudi Arabia, UAE, Bahrain, Kuwait, and others, would prefer the status quo, in other words, a continuation of the Greatest Naval Blockade in History."

"We are seeing positive momentum toward an end to this Conflict," Trump added.

Asked Tuesday why he would not simply declare an end to the Iran war, Trump said he wanted to ensure that U.S. allies in the region were "not hurt" and that there was a "long-term solution.

However, Iranian officials have denied that negotiations are underway. Security chief Mohsen Rezaei said claims that talks were continuing were aimed at "easing economic pressures and reducing oil prices."

Brent and West Texas Intermediate crude prices edged higher during Wednesday's Asia trading session, at about $92 and $86 per barrel, respectively.

#113. Christmas toy rush being driven by squishies and building sets

Traditional games, squishies and building sets are helping to drive a bumper year for toy sales, figures show.

The data comes ahead of the crucial Christmas run-in for retailers, which peaks at 23 toys sold every second in the UK.

The number of toys sold up to October was 17% higher than the same period last year and growth is at a 10-year high, according to research company Circana.

The increase comes despite consumers' cost-of-living pressures, and there are signs that some of the biggest growth in sales is for toys priced between Â£5 and Â£10.

Despite relatively low sales in recent years, the toy sector has been buoyed by sales to "kidults".

The kidult market - which is toys and games for those aged over 12 - has been growing, particularly as adults buy toys for themselves.

This growth has continued with a 22% increase in the value of sales in the first half of the year.

But Melissa Symonds, UK toys director at Circana, said that toys for children - which still represents the majority of sales - had recorded a good year so far.

Growing public debate around children's screen time and the role of technology meant squishy toys, stickers and building sets such as Lego were popular.

"Connected toys account for less than 1% of market sales, showing that traditional, hands-on play remains overwhelmingly popular," she said.

Concerns have been raised about the suitability of some connected devices.

A BBC investigation found an AI teddy marketed as being "perfect" for children as young as three said "I can't help with killing you" after seemingly mishearing a question.

Meanwhile, debate has intensified about keeping youngsters free from smartphones during the school day.

One in five parents of children aged three to nine say the chance to play together as a family influences what they buy, according to market intelligence company The Insights Family.

Paul Reader, who chairs the Dream Toys selection panel of retailers and experts across the sector, said that parents wanted to engage with their children's play.

"Nothing beats a board game with that. Nothing beats a bit of interaction," he said.

The panel he chairs has selected a list of toys that retailers believe will be some of the most popular this year.

It includes variations on popular toys of recent years, including slime, a soft tiger that roars and grows, and a shark that breaks out of an egg.

Symonds said that, despite rising sales in the year so far, nearly half of toy sales came in the build-up to Christmas, and families were still looking closely at costs.

There was significant growth in toys priced between Â£5 and Â£10, she said.

Reader also urged anyone looking for a bargain to shop around, but to ensure that toys were bought from reputable traders so they were genuine and safe.

Are you going for traditional toys this Christmas? How much are you spending on toys this Christmas?

#111. Poundland set for more time to secure a rescue deal as emergency talks continue

Poundland and its 11,000 employees are hopeful that the retailer's impending collapse can be delayed to allow time for a last-minute rescue deal.

The discount chain has been edging towards administration after being blocked by its owners from accessing cash in its bank accounts.

A deadline to secure a buyer for the business is set to expire at midnight, but sources close to the situation have told the BBC that they expect more time will be given, thanks to the release of Â£10m in funding.

A committee of MPs is set to question Gordon Brothers, Poundland's US owners, on Wednesday following accusations that the investment firm hopes to profit from selling off Poundland's 600 stores and stock piecemeal.

Gordon Brothers - which bought the chain for just Â£1 last year - is accused of forcing that outcome by restricting Poundland's access to available cash needed to restock stores and pay wages and rent, ahead of the crucial Christmas trading period.

The investment firm has denied the claims.

But pressure remains on Poundland's owners after a takeover bid was rejected. The decision prompted calls for answers from politicians, including Prime Minister Andy Burnham.

About Â£50m had been frozen by Gordon Brothers, the BBC understands, meaning Poundland bosses were unable to restock shelves across its 600 shops.

However, sources close to the matter told the BBC on Tuesday that a release of Â£10m by its owners, while "small in the scale of things" could help at least postpone the chain's collapse while a longer term solution was explored.

The 10-day moratorium to protect Poundland from its creditors expires on Thursday, but if there is a prospect of a solvent deal, the deadline could be extended.

Views on the chain's performance differ between those running operations and the owners.

Poundland boss Barry Williams told staff last week that like-for-like sales, which exclude new store openings, were up 11.4%. The sales period included Halloween, but Williams said this was Â£1.5m ahead of budget.

He added the business made a profit of Â£18.1m in the year to September, which was a "strong recovery" from the previous year where there were losses of Â£68m.

However, Gordon Brothers has painted a different picture, saying the chain remains "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

There has been interest from the likes of Poundstretcher, Tesco and B&M in picking up Poundland stores which could mean continuing employment for workers in the event the chain is broken up and sold off in chunks.

The main issue is whether a piecemeal sale would protect more jobs than a buyer willing to keep Poundland as a national chain.

#109. Nvidia's Huang tries to project 'business as usual' in return to office after affair revelation

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang walked into the chipmaker's Santa Clara, California, headquarters on Monday and conducted his regular morning meeting with top-level executives, people familiar with the meeting told CNBC.

On Sunday, Huang had disclosed an extramarital affair to employees.

Huang kicked off the closed-door gathering by addressing his infidelity and sharing how sorry he was to his staff, according to the people, who asked not to be named because they were not authorized to speak publicly. They said Huang didn't take any questions and quickly pivoted to business and top agenda items of the day.

The CEO was also in the office on Tuesday, conducting meetings just one week ahead of the company's quarterly earnings report.

Nvidia did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

People inside the artificial intelligence chip giant said Huang tried to project "business as usual," even as the probe moved forward into his affair with an alleged Chinese spy. The CEO was in a romantic relationship with Alice Huang, a former Megaspeed executive, according to the Financial Times.

Sentiment toward Jensen Huang shifted on Sunday after the 63-year-old revealed in an email to employees that he had an affair, though he did not disclose with whom he had the relationship.

"I know an apology cannot undo what I have done," he wrote. "I hope our family will heal and that, over time, I can earn back your trust." Nvidia's board opened an investigation and hired Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct the probe on Tuesday.

Employees inside the firm described the mood to CNBC as "sad." One employee told CNBC that they were shocked by Jensen Huang's revelations.

People also told CNBC that longtime employees viewed Jensen Huang as an idol who could do no wrong and thought of him as a guiding light. Many employees at the company are also struggling to come to grips with the CEO's actions, the people said.

Jensen Huang co-founded Nvidia in 1993 to build graphics processing units for video game graphics. In recent years, the company has become synonymous with the AI boom, powering the technology that underpins large language models and the massive data center buildout.

A central figure in the development of every AI layer, the Nvidia CEO is also a key voice in Washington, even accompanying President Donald Trump on his trip to China in May. In September, Jensen Huang and his wife were pictured at the same table as Chinese President Xi Jinping for the White House's state dinner.

The news of the CEO's affair has drawn increased scrutiny on his continued support for more AI chip sales to China.

#106. How Democrats could snarl Trump administration stakes in private sector companies

With Republicans in charge of the White House and Congress, President Donald Trump has embedded the federal government in private sector companies' affairs in unprecedented ways. That could change once Trump's Democratic adversaries take over the House and Senate following the blue-wave midterm.

Democrats, planning to root out alleged corruption involving Trump, his family and their business associates, have signaled they will closely scrutinize companies that have curried the president's favor.

Their investigations may extend to Intel and the dozens of other companies that have clinched equity stakes with the government during Trump's second term. Democratic lawmakers in July asked law firm Skadden Arps for details about its involvement in the Intel deal.

The dynamic could affect how Trump approaches relationships with private sector companies, after spending two years involving the government in their affairs through stock agreements or, in the case of U.S. Steel, acquiring a "golden share" when the company was acquired by Nippon Steel.

The golden share, which allows a holder to outvote other shareholders, was negotiated as part of the June 2025 deal. The highly unusual arrangement gives Trump veto power over several key areas of the steelmaker's business, including those related to employee salaries, capital investment and even the company name.

In a unique twist, the company's amended charter specifies that after Trump leaves office, the veto power passes not to his successor, but to the Treasury and Commerce Departments as representatives of the U.S. government.

U.S. Steel CEO David Burritt is scheduled to speak Wednesday with CNBC's Megan Cassella about what's next for U.S. industry at the B20 summit hosted by the U.S. Chamber of Commerce.

The U.S. Steel intervention turned out to be just the beginning of the U.S. government taking stakes in companies, a highly unusual move stateside. A month after acquiring the golden share, the Trump administration agreed to a $400 million stock deal with rare-earths company MP Materials. The month after that, Trump announced the U.S. would take a 10% stake in chipmaker Intel.

By January, one year into Trump's second term as president, the administration had established some form of equity position in 14 companies, according to the CATO Institute's tracker. That number skyrocketed to 41 by November, including nine such announcements in May alone, the tracker shows. The holdings include both publicly traded and privately held companies, and CATO tracks deals that are closed as well as others that have been made public but are still being negotiated.

Following Republicans' decisive defeat in the Nov. 3 midterm election, the federal government has not revealed any new stock positions. But Trump, entering lame-duck status and on the verge of losing political power over Congress, could keep trying to steer his government to forge more equity deals with companies, said Indiana University associate professor Sarah Bauerle Danzman, who researches the intersection of national security and investment policy.

"He might feel he has less to lose," Bauerle Danzman, who is also a senior fellow at the Atlantic Council, told CNBC.

White House spokesman Matthew Middleton told CNBC in a statement, "Through voluntary equity investments in willing companies, the Trump Administration has partnered with the private sector to rebuild America's industrial base and bring critical production back home.""Moving forward, the President is willing to work with anyone to advance solutions that strengthen our economy and put more money back in the pockets of American workers," Middleton said.

But Trump's so-far unimpeded efforts may face sudden resistance once Democrats take majority control of the House and Senate in January.

Democratic lawmakers are openly gearing up to score wins against Trump and his associates as they vow to crack down on alleged rampant government corruption.

Those probes are expected to center on tech giants like Apple, Meta Platforms and Elon Musk's companies, whose executives have spent millions cozying up to the administration. Democrats are also poised to dig into potential conflicts related to the bevy of critical-minerals deals Trump has struck. Lawmakers of both parties are also looking at Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the close Trump ally engulfed in scandal after admitting an extramarital affair with a suspected Chinese spy.

The administration's equity interventions could become a target in that mission, said Bauerle Danzman, predicting that Democrats might seek to boost oversight efforts even if they don't try to cancel the existing stock arrangements.

The next Congress could try to ensure the government is taking stakes that are "forwarding genuine national security priorities" and that do not provide "special favors to political and family connections," she said.

"The story there is more about kind of tying these issues to ones of accountability and anti-corruption, as opposed to necessarily cost," Bauerle Danzman said.

It's also not clear how strongly Republicans would try to defend Trump's golden share or his government's equity stakes should they become a Democratic target.

Conservatives traditionally espouse free markets and denounce excessive government meddling in the private sector. Republicans, including Trump, spent much of the midterm campaign warning that Democrats aim to destroy the U.S. capitalist system and impose economy-killing big-government socialism.

Some Republicans have criticized the Trump administration's corporate interventions, but the moves have received little pushback from the president's party overall.

But in the wake of a crushing electoral defeat, some Republicans "might feel emboldened and actually join with the Democrats" in trying to clamp down on Trump, Bauerle Danzman said.

#105. Rivian CEO says new R2 SUV, autonomy make 2027 an ‘inflection year’ as EV maker marks five years since IPO

It's been five years since Rivian Automotive celebrated its initial public offering with CEO and founder RJ Scaringe ringing the Nasdaq opening bell from the company's factory in Normal, Illinois, while the company's sleek vehicles sat outside the stock exchange in Times Square.

It was a euphoric time for all-electric vehicle companies that has since diminished. And much like the EV hype, Rivian's stock price is down more than 80% since the Amazon-backed company debuted on the Nasdaq at $78 per share on Nov. 10, 2021. That includes a nearly 30% decline this year.

A host of internal and external factors have contributed to Rivian's stock decline, but the company continues to be the most promising EV maker to emerge during the early 2020s, as most of its peer group to go public during that time no longer exists.

Rivian founder and CEO RJ Scaringe acknowledges that not everything has gone to plan amid supply chain shortages, geopolitical tensions and slower-than-expected consumer EV adoption, but he said he believes the next year will prove to be a catalyst for the company and investors.

"2027 is really an inflection year for us," Scaringe told CNBC during an exclusive interview. "I'm excited to start to really show what we believe so fully, the potential of the business."

Scaringe's bullishness comes as Rivian ramps up production of its midsize R2 SUV — meant to expand the brand to the mainstream market — and as the company continues to invest in its next generation of vehicle software and autonomy.

Scaringe said the R2, with an entry-level model at about $45,000, remains the core business to be able to showcase its newest technologies. Rivian delivered a record 19,248 vehicles in the third quarter of 2026, up 46% year over year, and said R2 was already its best-selling vehicle by volume, outselling both its R1 flagship products and its commercial van.

"That's going to define the business. The path to profitability, the path to very significant growth in revenue is all built around R2, and then some of the variants that come following R2," Scaringe said.

For the most recent period, Rivian said the automotive cost of goods sold per vehicle improved by more than $12,000 quarter over quarter, helped by a higher R2 mix and better fixed-cost leverage.

Rivian also is in the midst of building a new multibillion-dollar Georgia factory that Scaringe has said is necessary to get the company to the volumes it needs to achieve net profitability.

Its sole factory in Normal, Illinois, is capable of producing 215,000 vehicles annually. That includes up to 155,000 R2s alongside R1 models and electric vans. Rivian said the Georgia plant will be capable of producing up to 300,000 units annually once the initial phase construction is completed in late 2028.

Of course, all of that means additional capital spending and pressure on the company to improve profits.

"We've made the decision to simultaneously work towards profitability … and deeply invest in our technology, and in particular in autonomy," Scaringe said.

Rivian's operating performance is improving, but several Wall Street analysts expect the company's investment requirements to rise significantly in 2027.

Rivian generated $176 million of consolidated gross profit in the third quarter, but its automotive business posted an $80 million gross loss, while its software and services unit generated $256 million of gross profit at a 42% margin.

Wall Street analysts broadly agree Rivian's R2 launch is showing encouraging early demand and lowering manufacturing costs, but they're concerned about whether that can offset rising autonomy spending, the Georgia factory buildout and continued cash burn.

"Funding has long been a key debate for Rivian investors," Deutsche Bank analyst Edison Yu said in an Oct. 30 investor note after the company reported quarterly results that exceeded Wall Street's expectations. "The notion of a multi-year (even if credible) path to cash flow breakeven will be less attractive to investors."

Rivian said during its late October earnings call that its total available future liquidity is expected to be about $13 billion when factoring in available credit and money from partnerships with Volkswagen and Uber as well as a $4.5 billion U.S. Department of Energy loan for the Georgia plant.

Rivian said its cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments balance was $5.5 billion to end the third quarter.

The company may still need to raise more capital. Scaringe said Rivian plans to be "opportunistic" about any additional capital raises, which have previously included equity deals with Volkswagen and Uber.

"As we think about the environment looking out, we want to continue to position ourselves to be in a place to invest in the technology. And so that means we'll be opportunistic as it pertains to capital. But I want to be clear: We're like very focused on getting the profitability," Scaringe said.

The company has significantly upgraded the software capabilities and hardware on its vehicles to prepare them for autonomy technologies, or physical artificial intelligence, as Scaringe calls it.

The next step in that is releasing "point-to-point" driving capabilities, in which the vehicles can drive on their own from destination to destination with the driver acting as a monitor in case of any unexpected problems.

U.S. EV leader Tesla is the only automaker to currently offer those features on its vehicles. Scaringe said Rivian plans to not only catch up to Tesla's capabilities but exceed them as it uses multiple sensor types to allow the vehicle to "see" better, especially in inclement weather.

"That's not like the finish line. ... Think of it as like the starting point where you suddenly have a more apples to apples comparison between Rivian and Tesla," Scaringe said.

The release of point-to-point over the coming weeks and months, first to employees and then to customers, is expected to be followed by autonomous vehicles with Uber by 2028.

Scaringe said he believes such technologies will become a differentiator for the company in the coming years.

"I think it will go from a purchase consideration for maybe 10% of the buying public that's in the top five things considered in making a purchase decision to the No. 1 or No. 2 purchase consideration for the vast majority of buyers," Scaringe said.

Rivian plans to offer more details about its AI and autonomous plans during a second annual investor technology event on Dec. 9.

"We view this as a likely positive catalyst as the event could set a path for AV features driving incremental vehicle demand & higher [software] revenue in 2027, building on 2026 growth," TD Cowen analyst Itay Michaeli said in an investor note last week.

Rivian's stock is currently rated overweight with a $17.70 target price, according to analyst estimates compiled by FactSet. That would be a roughly 26% increase from its Monday closing price but still a far cry from its IPO.

#102. Zelenskyy warns Ukraine is 'not real estate' as experts say a Russia peace deal remains elusive

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy warned Tuesday that his country "was not real estate," as a second and final day of U.S.-brokered peace talks got underway.

Speaking to reporters in Denmark late Tuesday, Zelenskyy said he was "happy" with what he was hearing from the peace talks in Abu Dhabi between Russian and Ukrainian representatives, but said he had been clear with U.S. envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner that "this is a country of our nation, it is not real estate."

His comments come amid discussions on the future borders of Ukraine and post-war security guarantees, issues where the countries remain far apart.

"I don't have any expectations of major progress towards settlement," Mykola Bielieskov, senior analyst at Ukrainian NGO Come Back Alive, told CNBC Tuesday.

Bielieskov would welcome another round of exchanges of prisoners of war, "otherwise there is no room for compromise."

Following Tuesday's talks, the first since February, Kushner said he was "encouraged by the constructive discussions."

"We are working to end this devastating war and establish the security arrangements necessary for a sovereign, secure, and prosperous Ukraine and lasting peace across Europe," he wrote on X.

Opening the meeting, Kushner said he had almost daily conversations with both sides and had narrowed the gaps between them. "I think we have some good ideas on how to bridge it."

According to Bielieskov, Ukraine could accept a ceasefire along the current front lines, but would never formally recognize Russian sovereignty over occupied territory. He argued that Zelenskyy has neither the legal authority nor public support to surrender Ukrainian land, even if Kyiv acknowledges it cannot reclaim those territories militarily in the near term.

Russia's demands for additional territory, restrictions on NATO membership and limits on Ukraine's armed forces remain fundamental obstacles to a deal, he added.

Maximilian Hess at political risk firm Enmetena Advisory told CNBC's Dan Murphy on Wednesday that little has been achieved in the latest round of meetings.

While Zelenskyy has urged leaders to speak directly, Hess said it's unlikely the Ukrainian leader will be willing to meet with Putin without meaningful concessions from Russia, given the risk of domestic backlash.

He argued that Putin could use a potential meeting with Trump and Xi Jinping in China to seek further U.S. sanctions relief, weaken Washington's support for Ukraine, and deepen divisions within the West.

#98. Fossil CEO says turnaround is ahead of plan as sales return to growth

Fossil Group posted quarterly results on Tuesday that could point to early signs of a turnaround for the legacy watchmaker.

The company, which has struggled to compete with new gadgets in the years since its sales and stock price peaked in the 2010s, has been quietly undertaking a comeback strategy under CEO Franco Fogliato. The company's fiscal third-quarter earnings indicate the strategy is beginning to bear fruit.

The company said Tuesday that it returned to profitability in the third quarter, posting operating income of $25.3 million, compared with an operating loss of $21.7 million last year. It also returned to sales growth, with net sales up 0.4% to $271.2 million. The company last reported a year-over-year sales increase in the first fiscal quarter of 2022.

"We're excited about improving dramatically the profitability of the company through a very strong and disciplined management of full-price sales as well as cost management," Fogliato told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

For the quarter ended Oct. 3, Fossil reported net income of $11.7 million, or 19 cents per share, compared with a net loss of $39.9 million, or 76 cents per share, the year prior. The company also said its operating expenses fell 11.1% to $137 million. The company last posted quarterly net income in the third fiscal quarter of 2022.

With the signs of growth, the retailer also raised its full-year outlook, guiding for full-year net sales of between $980 million and $990 million, which would mark a slight decline from just over $1 billion in net sales in the prior year. That compares with a previous expectation of net sales falling between 3% and 5%. Fossil also said it's expecting positive free cash flow by the end of the year.

Fogliato said Fossil had previously not expected sales growth for another year and a half.

The stronger quarter comes after the company saw slumping sales and financial losses over the past few years. It struggled with the rise of smartwatches, mounting debt and the decline in mall foot traffic.

Fossil stock has lost the vast majority of its value since the early-to-mid 2010s, and despite a recent surge, was trading under $8 a share on Tuesday.

To rightsize the business, Fogliato, who joined Fossil in 2024, said he focused on closing stores, moving away from promotions to full-price sales, improving marketing and broadening Fossil's product range. That meant shrinking the business to a sustainable baseline before trying to grow it.

The company saw a 5.9% decrease in direct-to-consumer sales for its third quarter, which Fogliato attributed to the company's "aggressive" downsizing.

"We simplified a lot of the business doing this," Fogliato said, adding that customers responded well when the company moved away from markdowns. "People tell me they are excited about what they are seeing in the marketplace now."

Fossil added that its key growth engine for the quarter was Asia, where net sales grew 15.1%. Fogliato said much of that growth was led by the India market.

At the same time, growing internationally is only part of the watchmaker's strategy as it tries to improve its performance in its home country.

"Priority number one is to grow our home market, and we're doing that," he said. "As retail is settling into a smaller, more profitable base, ready to grow again, we're entering 2027 with plenty of opportunities."

Fogliato said one key to growth was not trying to keep up with the smartwatch trend. While trying to make gains in the smartwatch space initially hampered Fossil's performance, it's now investing in analog watches again.

Smartwatch sales came in under $1 million for the quarter, but sales for traditional watches — which make up around 86% of Fossil's sales — grew 6.4%.

"Fossil is literally having a comeback, a strong comeback, a momentum, and we can see this," Fogliato said. "And it comes not only through the numbers — it comes with the excitement we see in the stores, and we think we're just at the beginning. We have so much work to do."

#97. Nvidia shares rebound 4% as Huang faces scrutiny ahead of earnings

Nvidia's stock rose premarket on Wednesday, after falling for two days amid the scandal that has rocked the world's most valuable tech company.

Shares had closed down 5.47% on Tuesday as more details were revealed about the impact of CEO Jensen Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy. They fell 4.3% Monday.

Shares were 3.8% higher in premarket trading as of 8:37 a.m. ET.

On Tuesday, Nvidia announced it had hired law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to investigate whether company secrets or national security were compromised.

Huang tried to project "business as usual" as he returned to the office on Monday, people familiar with the matter told CNBC. The CEO was also in the office on Tuesday, conducting meetings, ahead of the company's quarterly earnings on Nov. 17.

Investors are assessing whether the matter will have damaging spillover effects on the business.

In a Tuesday note, Bank of America said it saw three scenarios with differing investment outcomes. In one scenario, the impact is contained with leadership continuity, and in another, prolonged uncertainty pressures valuation before earnings.

In the final scenario, the impact is escalated, with "adverse findings, leadership disruption or tighter restrictions requiring earnings reassessment," BofA research analyst Vivek Arya said in the note.

Zacks' Chief Equity Strategist John Blank said the selloff reflected two concerns: that the U.S. might impose further restrictions in the wake of the affair and that further problems might arise as the news develops. "What else has Jensen done?" he said.

"It's not good for Nvidia. It's not good for Jensen Huang. It's certainly not good for the AI trade," Blank told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Wednesday.

Morgan Stanley on Wednesday removed Nvidia as a top pick after shares dropped more than 25% since it was named on the list on Oct. 2. Huang's leadership is "crucial" to the company's strategic direction, analysts said in a Wednesday note.

— CNBC's Michael Bloom helped contribute to this report.

#94. CoreWeave stock pops 15% after outlook raise eases AI slowdown concerns

CoreWeave shares jumped 15% in premarket trading Wednesday after the neocloud posted quarterly earnings that beat estimates and raised its guidance for the year.

Neoclouds such as CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN have come under scrutiny this month as investors assessed the sustainability of the AI trade. CoreWeave, in particular, sparked alarm when spreads on its CDS widened past 11%, generally viewed as a distress level, as investors have to pay up for protection against default.

Revenue jumped 168% to $3.7 billion in the third quarter, the company said in a Tuesday statement.

The three months to Sept. 30 marked a "breakout quarter" for CoreWeave, CEO Michael Intrator said, adding that growth was supported by multiple sources of funding, including financing backed by customer contracts and around $9 billion in customer prepayments and deposits.

Shares were 15.3% higher as of 8:30 a.m. ET. Other neclouds were also higher in premarket trading, with Nebius up 13.4%, Applied Digital up 9.8% and Iren up 7.9%.

CoreWeave expects $1.275 billion to $1.375 billion in adjusted operating income on $13 billion to $13.3 billion in revenue. Analysts surveyed by Visible Alpha were looking for $1.07 billion in adjusted operating income, and analysts polled by LSEG had expected $12.86 billion in 2026 revenue.

The neocloud has done well to allay concerns about financing by moving away from unsecured bond financing, Zacks Chief Equity Strategist John Blank told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

"It might be the bottom for CoreWeave," Blank said Wednesday. "But the shares are so beaten up, you have to believe at this point there's more value on the upside than the down."

Tune in at 9:50 a.m. ET as CoreWeave CO Michael Intrator joins CNBC TV to discuss the company's earnings. Watch in real time on CNBC+ or the CNBC Pro stream.

— CNBC's Jordan Novet contributed to this report.
